24	\section{Structural Analysis Details}
25	\label{app:structural}
26	
27	Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.
28	
29	The temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $5{\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).
30	
31	\begin{table}[h]
32	\centering
33	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces baseline FPR below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching. Subscript $\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 injection seeds (baseline row only).}
34	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
35	\small
36	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
37	\toprule
38	Approach & Mechanism & Temporal FPR & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
39	\midrule
40	Baseline (external) & Cross-benchmark total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
41	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temporal DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
42	$\theta$-conditioning & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
43	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
44	\bottomrule
45	\end{tabular}
46	\end{table}

54	\caption{Cross-subject vs.\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal item instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",

    "oldString": "\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & ETS~C (\\%) & FPR (Li clean) & Enrichment \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subjects) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}",
    "newString": "\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\nA power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Item Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%.\n\nAcross all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & ETS~C (\\%) & FPR (Li clean) & Enrichment \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subjects) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nThe enrichment ratio of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} is 0.96 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}), indistinguishable from chance. This null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$, ${+}0.674$ at $p = 1.0$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). An item can be globally contaminated yet function identically if all cohorts have comparable access. The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method: the near-chance enrichment is expected, not evidence of failure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% (unmatched) to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$ vs.\\ $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.66$ for temporal cohorts), indicating ability confounding explains roughly half; the residual 28.3\\% is consistent with the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",

## 核心数据

METABENCH 的 Li et al. web-overlap contamination labels (9229 items) vs DIF-C flags 对比：
- Enrichment = **0.964** (DIF-C 率在 contaminated 30.7% vs clean 31.8% 几乎一致)
- Jaccard = **0.206** (两个 flagged 集合重叠极低)
- Phi = 0.012, Chi-squared = 1.28 (p=0.258), OR = 1.05 [0.96, 1.15]
- 按子类型一致：input_only enrichment=0.986, input_and_label enrichment=0.960

## 整合位置

1. **Claim 2 (§4 ground truth meta-critique)**: 从"概念论证"升级为"实证论证"。原文仅论证 web-overlap 和 DIF 测量不同维度，现在有 enrichment≈0.964 + Jaccard=0.206 的直接实证。加一段报告这些数据。
2. **Abstract**: 可加一句 "empirically validated by near-unity enrichment (0.964) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels"
3. **Introduction**: Claim 2 描述处加 "with empirical validation"
4. **Experiments section**: 加一个小段落或表格报告 plan_026 结果

数据来源：`artifacts/plan_026/constat_dif_comparison_report.md`

1	# Plan 026: DIF-C vs Web-Overlap Contamination — Empirical Comparison

5	- **Web-overlap labels**: Li et al. (2024, EMNLP) proxy contamination labels
6	- **Items**: 9229 annotated items (of 12508 total; 3279 lacked Li et al. annotation)
7

12	| **DIF-C**      | 1778       | 1119              | 2897 |
13	| **non-DIF**    | 3806       | 2526              | 6332 |
14	| **Total**      | 5584     | 3645            | 9229 |
15	
16	## Association Tests
17	
18	| Metric | Value | Interpretation |
19	|--------|-------|----------------|
20	| Chi-squared (df=1) | 1.2816 | p = 2.5761e-01 |
21	| Fisher exact test | OR = 1.0545 | p = 2.5139e-01 |
22	| Odds ratio [95% CI] | 1.0545 [0.9637, 1.1540] | >1: DIF-C slightly more common among web-contaminated |
23	| Phi coefficient | 0.0120 | Near zero = negligible association |
24	| Jaccard index | 0.2063 | Low overlap between flagged sets |
25	| % Agreement | 53.36% | |
26	
27	## Enrichment Analysis
28	
29	| Metric | Value |
30	|--------|-------|
31	| P(DIF-C ∣ web-contaminated) | 0.3070 (30.7%) |
32	| P(DIF-C ∣ web-clean) | 0.3184 (31.8%) |
33	| **Enrichment ratio** | **0.9642** |
34	
35	Enrichment ≈ 0.96 confirms plan_001 finding (0.96): DIF-C rates are virtually identical whether items are web-contaminated or clean.
36	
37	## Similarity Score Distribution (Continuous)
38	
39	| Group | n | Mean | Median | SD |
40	|-------|---|------|--------|----|
41	| DIF-C | 2897 | 0.5442 | 0.5112 | 0.3342 |
42	| non-DIF | 6332 | 0.5545 | 0.5293 | 0.3350 |
43	
44	| Test | Statistic | p-value | Effect size |
45	|------|-----------|---------|-------------|
46	| Mann-Whitney U | 8996452 | 1.3965e-01 | r = 0.0191 |
47	| Cohen's d | | | d = -0.0306 |
48	
49	## DIF-C Rate by Contamination Subtype
50	
51	| Contamination type | n items | n DIF-C | % DIF-C | Enrichment vs clean |
52	|-------------------|---------|---------|---------|---------------------|
53	| clean | 5584 | 1778 | 31.8% | 1.0000 |
54	| input_only | 602 | 189 | 31.4% | 0.9860 |
55	| input_and_label | 3043 | 930 | 30.6% | 0.9598 |
56	
57	## Web-Contamination Rate by ETS Class
58	
59	| ETS class | n items | n web-contam | % web-contam |
60	|-----------|---------|-------------|-------------|
61	| A | 4710 | 1901 | 40.4% |
62	| B | 1622 | 625 | 38.5% |
63	| C | 2897 | 1119 | 38.6% |
64	
65	## Interpretation
66	
67	1. **Enrichment ≈ 0.96**: The probability of an item being DIF-C is essentially the same regardless of web-overlap contamination status. This directly demonstrates that DIF captures a fundamentally different signal than web-overlap detection methods.
68	
69	2. **Jaccard = 0.2063**: The two flagged sets (DIF-C and web-contaminated) have very low overlap, confirming they identify different item populations.
70	
71	3. **Phi = 0.0120**: The correlation between DIF-C status and web-contamination status is negligible (near zero).
72	
73	4. **Effect size negligible** (d = -0.0306): Even using continuous similarity scores, DIF-C and non-DIF items show virtually no difference in web-overlap severity.
74	
75	**Conclusion**: Web-overlap contamination labels and differential item functioning capture orthogonal signals. Using web-overlap as "ground truth" for benchmark contamination is a category error — DIF measures differential performance across model cohorts, which web-overlap methods cannot detect.
76

### Tool result

_source line 3079 · 2026-05-21T08:30:49.192Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HLiCFDxSf1ge3aWHb7vmbR`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\%) of the instability; 92.4\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning, and the enrichment between DIF flags and these labels is 0.96 (chance level). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$). DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal); an exploratory analysis ($N = 33$) finds they anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$), suggesting that DIF captures genuine capability change rather than memorization. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/abstract.tex",
      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\\%) of the instability; 92.4\\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning, and the enrichment between DIF flags and these labels is 0.96 (chance level). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$). DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal); an exploratory analysis ($N = 33$) finds they anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$), suggesting that DIF captures genuine capability change rather than memorization. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
      "numLines": 4,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 4
    }
  },

38	
39	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
40	\label{sec:groundtruth}
41	
42	The enrichment ratio of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} is 0.96 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}), indistinguishable from chance. This null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$, ${+}0.674$ at $p = 1.0$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). An item can be globally contaminated yet function identically if all cohorts have comparable access. The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method: the near-chance enrichment is expected, not evidence of failure.
43	
44	\begin{figure}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}

48	\label{fig:dose_response}

19	We do not modify the MH-DIF procedure itself; the contributions are empirical, not algorithmic. Three layers of genuine novelty emerge: (a)~we discover that 31\% temporal instability is robust across thresholds and unrelated to capability improvement, the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models; (b)~we expose a fundamental validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature, showing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; and (c)~we provide a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K with independent contamination validation through the GSM1K held-out set~\citep{zhang2024gsm1k}. We make the following contributions:
20	
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	\item \textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification, with C\% ranging from 21\% to 48\% across all 57 subjects (mean $\approx 30\%$) and confirmed non-random by random-split control at 0.18\% (\Cref{sec:temporal}).
23	
24	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. The enrichment between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is 0.96 (chance level), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
25	
26	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
27	
28	\item \textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal) and anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, suggestive at $N = 33$), indicating that DIF captures item-level behavioral change distinct from simple memorization (\Cref{sec:downstream,sec:gsm8k}).
29	\end{enumerate}
30	
```

The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\nA power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Item Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%.\n\nAcross all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nThe enrichment ratio of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} is 0.96 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}), indistinguishable from chance. This null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$, ${+}0.674$ at $p = 1.0$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). An item can be globally contaminated yet function identically if all cohorts have comparable access. The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method: the near-chance enrichment is expected, not evidence of failure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% (unmatched) to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$ vs.\\ $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.66$ for temporal cohorts), indicating ability confounding explains roughly half; the residual 28.3\\% is consistent with the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "newString": "We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$).",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), and find that 31\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\\%) of the instability; 92.4\\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning, and the enrichment between DIF flags and these labels is 0.96 (chance level). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$). DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal); an exploratory analysis ($N = 33$) finds they anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$), suggesting that DIF captures genuine capability change rather than memorization. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. The enrichment between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is 0.96 (chance level), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena, and the near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is the expected outcome of this mismatch.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about MMLU's measurement properties.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nWe do not modify the MH-DIF procedure itself; the contributions are empirical, not algorithmic. Three layers of genuine novelty emerge: (a)~we discover that 31\\% temporal instability is robust across thresholds and unrelated to capability improvement, the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models; (b)~we expose a fundamental validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature, showing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; and (c)~we provide a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K with independent contamination validation through the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k}. We make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification, with C\\% ranging from 21\\% to 48\\% across all 57 subjects (mean $\\approx 30\\%$) and confirmed non-random by random-split control at 0.18\\% (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. The enrichment between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is 0.96 (chance level), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal) and anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, suggestive at $N = 33$), indicating that DIF captures item-level behavioral change distinct from simple memorization (\\Cref{sec:downstream,sec:gsm8k}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena, and the near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is the expected outcome of this mismatch.

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena, and the near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is the expected outcome of this mismatch.",
    "newString": "Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena, and the near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is the expected outcome of this mismatch.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about MMLU's measurement properties.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nWe do not modify the MH-DIF procedure itself; the contributions are empirical, not algorithmic. Three layers of genuine novelty emerge: (a)~we discover that 31\\% temporal instability is robust across thresholds and unrelated to capability improvement, the first large-scale characterization of item-level benchmark stability at 5,227 models; (b)~we expose a fundamental validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature, showing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; and (c)~we provide a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K with independent contamination validation through the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k}. We make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification, with C\\% ranging from 21\\% to 48\\% across all 57 subjects (mean $\\approx 30\\%$) and confirmed non-random by random-split control at 0.18\\% (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal) and anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, suggestive at $N = 33$), indicating that DIF captures item-level behavioral change distinct from simple memorization (\\Cref{sec:downstream,sec:gsm8k}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels exposes a broader gap: all prior work validating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints include: (1) reliable detection requires $N \\geq 80$ per cohort; (2) ability differences confound the DIF signal (within-family C\\% $= 61.3\\%$, reduced to 28.3\\% after ability matching); and (3) MH detects only uniform DIF, making the 31\\% estimate conservative (24.8\\% additional non-uniform DIF at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$). Key limitations include inability to distinguish contamination from capability change and limited benchmark scope (MMLU + GSM8K).\n\nThese findings motivate a benchmark maintenance protocol: periodic DIF monitoring across model cohorts, item-level stability labels (stable/drifting/unstable) published alongside benchmark releases, and cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for semi-synthetic validation. _source line 3145 · 2026-05-21T08:33:35.122Z_

[Director 指令] **[Patch — 重要新结果] plan_027 temporal cohort ability overlap 完成**

这是直接回应审稿人 "ability confound 可能膨胀 31.3%" 的关键证据。优先整合。

## 核心数据

**Temporal cohort ability gap 很小：**
- Cohen's d = -0.17（negligible），overlap coefficient = 76.4%
- 对比 Llama-2 vs 3: d = -0.99，overlap = 17.9%

**Ability-matched C% 稳定：**

| Restriction | N_ref | N_foc | Cohen's d | C% |
|-------------|-------|-------|-----------|----|
| Full population | 1080 | 807 | -0.173 | 31.3 |
| Range overlap | 1080 | 787 | -0.149 | 30.9 |
| NN matching (5 seeds) | 638 | 638 | -0.010 | 28.9 ± 0.1 |

**关键对比：**
- Llama-2 vs 3: 61.3% → 28.3%（matching 减少 54%）→ DIF 被 ability confound 膨胀
- Temporal cohort: 31.3% → 28.9%（matching 仅减少 7.7%）→ 基本是 genuine DIF

## 整合位置

1. **§4.2 (Temporal Comparability)**: 加一段报告 ability overlap (d=-0.17, OVL=76.4%) 和 matched C%=28.9%±0.1%。这直接回应 "MH matching 在大能力跳跃下失效" 的审稿意见——temporal cohort 的能力跳跃很小。
2. **§4.x (Within-Family)**: 更新 Llama-2v3 讨论，对比 d=-0.99→matched 28.3% vs temporal d=-0.17→matched 28.9%。结论：两个 comparison 在 ability matching 后收敛到相同水平 (~28-29%)，说明 genuine item-level DIF 约 28-29%，Llama 的额外 33pp 来自 ability confound。
3. **Discussion**: 加一句 "Temporal cohort ability gap is negligible (d=-0.17); ability-matched C% confirms 31.3% is not inflated by impact confounding."

数据来源：`artifacts/plan_027_temporal_overlap/temporal_overlap_report.md`

不用验证引用。

```text
11	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Item Instability}
12	\label{sec:temporal}
13	
14	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
15	
16	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\%.
17	
18	Across all 57 MMLU subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\% as a robustness check (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\% C\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\% $= 48.9\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.0$, $18.3\%$ at $\geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$.
```

</details>

```text
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\% at $|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), making the 31\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\% vs.\ random 26.8\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\% vs.\ 30.9\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% (unmatched) to $28.3\% \pm 1.6\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$ vs.\ $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.66$ for temporal cohorts), indicating ability confounding explains roughly half; the residual 28.3\% is consistent with the temporal rate of 31.3\%. Full details in \Cref{app:structural}.
```

  "new_string": "Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \\approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$---a 7.7\\% relative reduction from 31.3\\%, confirming that at most $\\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch."
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\nA power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Item Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%.\n\nAcross all 57 MMLU subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\\% as a robustness check (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% (unmatched) to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$ vs.\\ $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.66$ for temporal cohorts), indicating ability confounding explains roughly half; the residual 28.3\\% is consistent with the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",

This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% (unmatched) to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$ vs.\\ $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.66$ for temporal cohorts), indicating ability confounding explains roughly half; the residual 28.3\\% is consistent with the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$ under external matching (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory analysis, the GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\\% at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 pp of 31.3\\% C\\%; 92.4\\% is item-specific. This does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: all prior work validating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning.
